- **Commands**: the host handle's `dispatchCommand` goes to the engine for engine nodes and through Fabric (`nativeFabricUIManager.dispatchCommand`, React Native's `DispatchCommandMountItem`, the library's `receiveCommand`) for a foreign node and for everything inside one (`State.foreign`). The same for `setNativeProps`. `WebViewHandle` and `MapHandle` (`foreign.ts`) wrap the commands of the two libraries (the map's Fabric commands take JSON strings).
- **Events** arrive through React Native's normal Fabric path (`EventEmitterWrapper` on the view state, `registerEventHandler`), named by the view config's registration names; nothing is engine code. The WebView is the exception on Android: `RNCWebView` reports `postMessage` calls and navigation requests through the JavaScript module `RNCWebViewMessagingModule` (`getJSModule`), tagged with the instance's `messagingModuleName`, and blocks the navigation (up to 250 ms) until `RNCWebViewModule.shouldStartLoadWithLockIdentifier` answers. `listenToWebView` registers that module (`RN$registerCallableModule`) and `WebView.svelte` answers the requests with react-native-webview's own rules (origin whitelist, `onShouldStartLoadWithRequest`, external schemes to the system).
- **Maps** mount their children after `onMapReady`, as react-native-maps' own component does: Fabric inserts a child before its parent, the native map creates its marker layer when the Google map is ready, and a marker that arrives earlier throws.

### What stays off the start

React Native builds the view managers of every package that does not offer them on demand when the host starts, on the JavaScript thread; react-native-maps creates a dozen, and Expo builds the definition of every module that is not in the lazy list. The foreign packages therefore stay off the start:

- `androidOnDemandViewManagers` (the Expo config plugin, `app.config.ts`): `MapsPackage` and `RNCWebViewPackage` are wrapped in a generated `SvelteNativeOnDemandPackage` (`ViewManagerOnDemandReactPackage`; TurboModules served as before; the package's managers are created together on the first request by name). A test reads the names back from the packages' sources.
- `expo-video` (and, since 2 November 2026, `expo-blur`, `expo-conic-gradient`, `expo-image` and `expo-linear-gradient`: see "Boot on the phone, round 2") is in the lazy module list of `patches/expo-modules-core@57.0.23.patch`. Its view hooks are served by `ExpoViews`: `registerForeign("ViewManagerAdapter_ExpoVideo_...")` builds the module, registers the Fabric components of its views (`FabricComponentsRegistry`, which adds to the shared provider registry and to every existing component registry), and `ExpoViewsPackage` (added to the package list by the plugin) answers React Native's on-demand lookup with the module's view manager. Its foreground and background hooks act on later transitions only, which is all they did for a module created after start.

Measured on the OVH emulator with `native-qa boot --warm 6 --cold 0`, a build without the three packages (`x-base`, APK 33.8 MB) against the final build (`x-fv14`, 38.8 MB), medians of the launches. The base's five runs were taken over the afternoon, the last two interleaved with the final build's two (base, final, base, final).

| | launches | `Running "main"` | Displayed (Start proc) | `[Svelte boot]` total |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| no foreign packages | 30 (5 runs) | 570 ms | 749 ms | 208 ms |
| packages started eagerly (first build, before the wrappers) | 6 | 604 ms | 738 ms | 178 ms |
| on demand (final) | 12 (2 runs) | 595 ms | 738 ms | 160 ms |

The first build, with the packages started eagerly, cost 35 ms of `Running "main"` over the base. The cause: React Native's JavaScript reads one `UIManager` property per view manager name a package announces, and each read creates that manager, so announcing the names was the cost. With empty announced names (`SvelteNativeOnDemandPackage`, `ExpoViewsPackage`) nothing of the three packages runs at start. The remaining difference in `Running "main"` is 25 ms (4 %) and lies inside the spread of one APK between runs (the 6-launch medians of the on-demand builds are 539 to 610 ms, the base's 561 to 610 ms); the displayed time and the JavaScript total do not move. The phone (Samsung A16) was not measured.



### Native-view libraries

Six more libraries mount their native views the same way (written 28 Oct 2026, branch `feat/svelte-native-views`). Each has a Svelte component in `@rasheed/svelte-native/foreign` that ports what the library's React code does before the native view (source and style normalisation, colour conversion, event payloads, commands) and mounts the view by name; none imports the library or React (`tests/foreign-libraries.test.ts` bundles the entry and fails on either). The versions are Expo SDK 57's: expo-image 57.0.5, expo-blur 57.0.3, expo-linear-gradient 57.0.2, react-native-video 6.19.3, @react-native-community/datetimepicker 9.1.0, lottie-react-native 7.3.8.

| Library | Svelte | Native view | Android | iOS | Not ported |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| expo-image | `ExpoImage` (`source`, `placeholder`, `contentFit`, `contentPosition`, `transition`, `tintColor`, `cachePolicy`, `priority`, `recyclingKey`, `blurRadius`, `autoplay`, `allowDownscaling`, `decodeFormat`, `onLoadStart/onLoad/onProgress/onError/onLoadEnd/onDisplay`) | `ViewManagerAdapter_ExpoImage` (the module's only view; config from `expo.getViewConfig`, `tintColor` named as a colour) | the view; `source` and `placeholder` as arrays (URL, `{ uri, headers, cacheKey }`, `require`d asset through `resolveAssetSource`, blurhash, thumbhash, a loaded image's shared-object id), `resizeMode`/`fadeDuration`/`defaultSource` mapped as the wrapper does; our own `Image` is unchanged | same props plus SF symbols (`sf:` sources, `symbolWeight`, `symbolSize`, `sfEffect`); not compiled or run | `prefetch`, `loadAsync`, the cache functions and the view's `startAnimating`/`stopAnimating`/`lockResourceAsync`/`unlockResourceAsync`/`reloadAsync` (reachable through `requireNativeModule("ExpoImage")`), `ImageBackground`, `useImage` |
| expo-blur | `BlurView` (`intensity`, `tint`, `blurReductionFactor`, `blurMethod`, deprecated `experimentalBlurMethod`, `blurTarget`), `BlurTargetView` | `ViewManagerAdapter_ExpoBlur_ExpoBlurView`, `..._ExpoBlurTargetView` | `blurMethod="none"` (default) draws the translucent tint and needs nothing else. `dimezisBlurView`/`dimezisBlurViewSdk31Plus` render what a `BlurTargetView` holds: pass its `ref` handle as `blurTarget`; the tag reaches the blur view with the update after the first commit. The target is a foreign view, so its children are mounted by React Native: they draw and lay out, and take no JavaScript touch or responder events | blurs what is behind the view; `BlurTargetView` is a plain view; not compiled or run | `blurTarget` as a React ref object (a handle is given), the web fallback |
| expo-linear-gradient | `LinearGradient` (`colors`, `locations`, `start`, `end`, `dither`) | `ViewManagerAdapter_ExpoLinearGradient` | a container that carries the style and the children, with the gradient filling it and the style's corner radii as `borderRadii`, as the library's wrapper builds it | the gradient view is the container | the web fallback |
| react-native-video | `Video` (`source` with headers, DRM, ads, text tracks, buffer config; `paused`, `muted`, `repeat`, `resizeMode`, `rate`, `volume`, `controls`, `poster`, track selections, every callback of the library) | `RCTVideo`, a legacy view manager (`requireNativeComponent`, no codegen) mounted through React Native's Fabric interop; its static config is compared with `VideoNativeProps` and with the Android manager's `@ReactProp`s and event names | ExoPlayer view. The ref's `seek`, `pause`, `resume`, `setVolume`, `setFullScreen`, `setSource`, `getCurrentPosition` and the PiP functions call the library's own `VideoManager` module with the view's tag (the library does not use view commands) | same props; `save` and the iOS-only props are in the config; not compiled or run | `renderLoader`, FairPlay's `getLicense` answer path (`setLicenseResultCmd`), `restoreUserInterfaceForPictureInPictureStopCompleted`, the web component |
| @react-native-community/datetimepicker | iOS `DateTimePicker` (controlled: `value`, `mode`, `display`, `minimumDate`, `maximumDate`, `minuteInterval`, `locale`, `textColor`, `accentColor`, `themeVariant`, `onValueChange(event, date)`, `onDismiss`, deprecated `onChange`), `openDatePicker`/`dismissDatePicker` | iOS `RNDateTimePicker` (Fabric); Android has no view: its pickers are the TurboModules `RNCDatePicker`, `RNCTimePicker`, `RNCMaterialDatePicker`, `RNCMaterialTimePicker` | `openDatePicker({ value, mode: "date" \| "time", minimumDate, maximumDate, is24Hour, minuteInterval, display, design: "material", ... }) => Promise<Date \| null>` opens the dialog and answers with the date, or null when it is dismissed or its neutral button is pressed; the `DateTimePicker` component mounts nothing there and warns | the component; `openDatePicker` rejects (no module); not compiled or run | the callback API of `DateTimePickerAndroid.open` (the promise replaces it), Windows |
| lottie-react-native | `LottieView` (`source`, `autoPlay`, `loop`, `speed`, `duration`, `progress`, `resizeMode`, `renderMode`, `colorFilters`, text filters, Android switches, `onAnimationFinish/Failure/Loaded`) | `LottieAnimationView` (codegen Fabric component; its static config is compared with the library's spec) | `source` as a name, `{ uri }`, a `require`d `.lottie` asset or the animation object, as `parsePossibleSources` resolves it (compared with the library's own function); `play(start?, end?)`, `pause()`, `resume()`, `reset()` are Fabric commands; `autoPlay` plays from the ref as the library does | same | the web-only props (`hover`, `webStyle`), `containerStyle` |

One pattern for the date picker on both platforms:

```svelte
{#if Platform.OS === "ios"}
  <DateTimePicker {value} mode="date" display="compact" onValueChange={(_, date) => (value = date)} />
{:else}
  <Pressable onPress={async () => { value = (await openDatePicker({ value, mode: "date" })) ?? value; }}><Text>{value.toDateString()}</Text></Pressable>
{/if}
```

**Props with processors** (`fabric/view-configs.ts`): a view config's attribute can now convert a list of colours (`colorLists`: the gradient's `colors`) and name the colour field of each struct in an array (`colorStructs`: Lottie's `colorFilters` are `{ keypath, color }`); `shutterColor`, `tintColor`, `accentColor`, `textColor` are colours; images go through `resolveAssetSource` in the components (`source`, `placeholder`, `poster`) because the native views take resolved URIs. `expoViewName(module, view?, kind)` takes the kind of the props the module's config does not tell from plain ones.

**On demand on Android.** `androidOnDemandViewManagers` lists `ReactVideoPackage` (`RCTVideo`) and `LottiePackage` (`LottieAnimationView`) next to the maps and WebView packages; `ReactVideoPackage` builds its `VideoManager` with `createNativeModules`, which the `BaseReactPackage` wrapper would hide, so the generated `svelteNativeOnDemand(...)` picks `SvelteNativeOnDemandLegacyPackage` for any package that is not a `BaseReactPackage` (a test reads the names and the package kinds back from the libraries' sources). The datetimepicker package has TurboModules only: no view manager. expo-image, expo-blur and expo-linear-gradient are Expo modules that are **not** in the expo-modules-core patch's lazy list, so Expo builds them and exports their views when the host starts (as in any Expo app); `ExpoViews.prepare` leaves them alone and builds views only for the modules the patch defers (`ExpoVideo`). Adding the three to `LAZY_MODULE_NAMES` (none has foreground, intent or user-leave hooks) would keep them off the start too, but changes the patch and the lockfile's patch hash, and the start cost has to be measured on a device first.

**Lab.** `/__dev/foreign?mode=libraries` (a section per library), `?mode=libraries-list` (one library per row of the windowed `List`) and `?mode=libraries-sheet` (all six in a native sheet), driven by `src/lib/native/dev/LibrarySet.svelte`; the video is the lab's 4 KB data-URI mp4, the image a 15 KB bundled PNG (`assets/native/lab/lab-image.png`), the animation a hand-written object (`lab-lottie.ts`). Events are recorded under the set's name (`record`) and the video's, the animation's and the date dialog's functions are in `libraryHandles`. The route stays under `notCovered` in `qa/lib/screens.ts`: the content moves (the looping animation, video frames) or is the libraries' own rendering.

**Verified**: `bun test` in `packages/svelte-native` (nodes, view names, payloads, commands and events per library against a fake Fabric binding; the libraries' own `resolveSources`, `resolveContentFit`, `parsePossibleSources` run on the same inputs; the static configs against the codegen specs and the Android manager's props and events; the on-demand names), typecheck, lint, and the Android release build (`native-qa build --abi x86_64`: prebuild with all six, 0 `e:` lines). **Not verified**: anything on a device or an emulator (rendering, touch, scrolling in a row or a sheet, the blur target's tag, `VideoManager` through the foreign tree, ExoPlayer on a `data:` URI, Lottie playback), and iOS entirely (nothing compiled or run there).

### Six more native-view libraries

Written 28 Oct 2026 (branch `feat/svelte-native-views`, second lane). Same structure as the six above: a component in `@rasheed/svelte-native/foreign` that ports what the library's JavaScript does before the native view, the view mounted by name, static view configs in `view-configs.ts`, no React and none of the libraries' modules in the bundle (`tests/foreign-libraries.test.ts` fails on either). The versions are the ones a production app on Expo SDK 57 and React Native 0.86 uses: react-native-pdf 7.0.3 with react-native-blob-util 0.24.9, react-native-view-shot 5.1.0, rive-react-native 9.7.1, expo-conic-gradient 0.2.1, react-native-fast-shadow 0.1.1, react-native-animateable-text 0.19.3.

| Library | Svelte | Native view | Android | iOS | Not ported |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| react-native-pdf | `Pdf` (`source`, `page`, `scale`, `minScale`, `maxScale`, `horizontal`, `enablePaging`, `fitPolicy`, `spacing`, `password`, `enableRTL`, `enableAnnotationRendering`, `enableAntialiasing`, `scrollEnabled`, `singlePage`, `trustAllCerts`, `onLoadComplete(pages, path, size, tableContents)`, `onPageChanged`, `onError`, `onPressLink`, `onPageSingleTap`, `onScaleChanged`, a `loading` snippet; `ref.setPage(page)`) | `RNPDFPdfView`, a codegen component; its config is compared with the library's Flow spec and the Android manager's `@ReactProp`s | AndroidPdfViewer. The view takes a local `path`, so the component does `Pdf._loadFromSource`/`_prepareFile` through react-native-blob-util's TurboModule (`foreign-pdf.ts`): a cached file that has not expired is used; an `http(s)` URI is fetched with the source's method, headers and body to `<cache>/<sha1(uri)>.pdf.tmp`, checked against `Content-Length` (skipped for an encoded response) and moved; `bundle-assets://` is copied; `data:application/pdf;base64,` is written; any other URI is a decoded path. The result is ignored when the source changed meanwhile and the download is cancelled. The one string event (`onChange`: `loadComplete\|pages\|w\|h\|toc`, `pageChanged`, `error`, `pageSingleTap`, `scaleChanged`, `linkPressed`) is read as `Pdf._onChange` does; `setPage` is the `setNativePage` command | PDFKit view (`usePDFKit`'s default); not compiled or run | the download progress (`onLoadProgress`, `renderActivityIndicator`, `progressContainerStyle`), `usePDFKit={false}` (the JavaScript page renderers of iOS), Windows |
| react-native-view-shot | `captureRef(handle, options)`, `captureScreen`, `releaseCapture`, `ViewShot` (`options`, `captureMode` mount or continuous, `onCapture`, `onCaptureFailure`; `ref.capture()`) | none: the library's TurboModule `RNViewShot` takes a React tag and draws that view | **Needs an engine hook, written** (below). The module finds the view through `FabricUIManager.addUIBlock` and React Native's mounting manager, which has never seen an engine view, so it fails with "No view found" for every engine tag; `SvelteEngine.captureView` draws those | the module finds React Native's own views by tag; the engine does not replace that mounting on iOS, so the host's handles work as they are; not compiled or run | `captureMode="update"` (it follows React's re-renders); for engine views `raw`, `zip-base64` and `snapshotContentContainer` (rejected with a message; the module still captures foreign views with them, and every option) |
| rive-react-native | `Rive` (`resourceName`, `url`, `source`, `artboardName`, `animationName`, `stateMachineName`, `autoplay`, `fit`, `alignment`, `layoutScaleFactor`, `referencedAssets`, `dataBinding`, `onPlay`, `onPause`, `onStop`, `onLoopEnd`, `onStateChanged`, `onRiveEventReceived`, `onError`; children behind the animation; `ref`: `play`, `pause`, `stop`, `reset`, `fireState`, `setInputState` and the at-path forms, the state getters, `touchBegan`/`touchEnded`, text runs, the data-binding setters, `trigger`) | `RiveReactNativeView`, a legacy view manager like `RCTVideo`; props compared with the Android manager's `@ReactProp`s and the iOS manager's exported properties, events with the `Events` enum and the iOS `RCTDirectEventBlock`s | `UIManager.dispatchViewManagerCommand(tag, name, args)` is the host's `dispatchCommand(name, args)` (Fabric `DispatchStringCommandMountItem` to `receiveCommand`); events `onPlay` reach JavaScript as `topPlay`; the state getters call `RiveReactNativeModule` with the tag | same through the interop's `RCT_EXTERN_METHOD`s; not compiled or run | the property listeners (`useRiveNumber` and the other hooks subscribe through a device event emitter), `RiveRenderer.defaultRenderer` |
| expo-conic-gradient | `ConicGradient` (`colors`, `locations`, `center`, `angle`) | `ViewManagerAdapter_ConicGradient`, the module's only view (config from `expo.getViewConfig`, the colour list named) | the view draws its gradient over its own children, so the gradient fills a container that carries the style and the children (the engine's views, drawn above it) | SwiftUI view, no children: the same container | the web fallback |
| react-native-fast-shadow | `ShadowedView`, `shadowStyle` | `FastShadowView` (a `ReactViewManager`: shadow, background, border and radii in one view); config compared with the manager's `@ReactProp`s | `shadowColor`, `shadowOpacity`, `shadowRadius`, `shadowOffset` of the style and the `cornerRadii` the wrapper reads from it (`getCornerRadiiFromStyle`, run against the library's own on the same styles, RTL too). The native view is the box itself, so its children would be React Native's and hear no touches in JavaScript: instead it fills a container behind the engine's children and takes the background, border and radii, `pointerEvents: "none"`, while the container keeps the layout (`shadowedParts`). The container keeps the border widths, and Yoga places an absolute child inside the padding box, so the native view is inset by the widths, negative (`borderInsets`, resolved as Yoga resolves a border edge: a side over `borderWidth`, start and end over left and right by the layout direction), and covers the border box instead of leaving a transparent ring; `tests/foreign-shadow-geometry.test.ts` compiles React Native's Yoga for the host and checks it for twelve spellings of a border, both directions. `overflow: "hidden"` on the ShadowedView clips its own shadow, as on iOS: put it on the child | a plain `View`, as the library's `ShadowedView` is there | the corner radii of the single child's style (the library's fallback), animated and shared-value styles (not split between the two views) |
| react-native-animateable-text | `AnimateableText` (`text`, `animatedProps`, the `Text` props) | `JBAnimatedText`, a text view with a C++ shadow node (`CParagraphComponentDescriptor`, autolinked) whose string is the `text` prop; the config is the library's own `validAttributes` | `animatedProps` is a worklet returning `{ text }`; see below | not compiled or run | `onPress`, `onLongPress` and the touchable highlight, nested text and children |

**`SvelteEngine.captureView(tag, options)`** (`Capture.kt`) is the one engine hook of this lane. React Native's own path to a view by tag cannot find engine views (the same reason `dispatchCommand` is an engine function, "Commands"), and `react-native-view-shot` has no other way in: its `ViewShot` class is driven by `FabricUIManager.addUIBlock`. `foreign-capture.ts` asks the engine first on Android; the engine draws the view of its registry into a bitmap (`png`, `jpg`, `webm`; `tmpfile`, `base64`, `data-uri`; `width` and `height`, `quality`, `fileName`, `handleGLSurfaceViewOnAndroid`; how it is drawn is below) and writes the same files the library does (the `ReactNative-snapshot-image` prefix in the cache directory, so the library's `releaseCapture` deletes them too; the library's clean-up on exit runs only if its own module was instantiated, which an app that captures only engine views may never have done, so the engine deletes the files it made when its module is destroyed, `OnDestroy`), and it answers null for a tag that is not an engine view of its own: a foreign root's frame (`EngineForeign`), anything inside a foreign view, an unknown tag. The library's module then draws those, with its `SurfaceView` and `TextureView` handling and with every option (the engine looks the view up first and refuses an option only for an engine view: `raw`, `zip-base64` and `snapshotContentContainer`; `CaptureRules.plan`, checked on the host by `src/test/run.sh`). The options are validated first, as the library's `validateOptions` does (its output is compared with ours on twelve inputs on both platforms in `tests/foreign-capture.test.ts`; the library names the default it fell back to in its format and result messages, we name the value that was given). A `View` with nothing to draw is flattened away by Fabric unless it says `collapsable={false}`; `ViewShot` does.

**How `Capture.kt` draws** (recording on the main thread, the rest off it; `CaptureGpu.kt`). On API 29+ the view is recorded into a `RenderNode` (`beginRecording`, `View.draw(recordingCanvas)`; the view's own `clipToOutline` outline is put on the node, because `View.draw` leaves out what its parent's node applies), synced with `HardwareRenderer.syncAndDraw()` into an `ImageReader` surface (the light is the theme's usual one: ambient 0.039, spot 0.19, 800 dp up and 200 dp over the top edge; the theme's own attributes are not public) and the hardware buffer is copied to an ARGB_8888 bitmap off the main thread (from API 33 after waiting on the image's fence). That path applies what a software `View.draw` loses: the outline clip of the engine's rounded views (`Props.kt`'s `RoundedOutline`, `EngineImage`'s rounded corners), elevation shadows, and `TextureView` children (Rive, video or Lottie with a texture), which draw nothing on a software canvas. On API < 29, when the GPU path throws, is refused (`syncAndDraw` result) or does not deliver in 3 s, and whenever the tree holds a `SurfaceView`, the view is drawn with `View.draw` into a software canvas on the main thread and the library's `TextureView` walk copies each visible texture in at its place (`getBitmap`, then the ancestors' position, scroll, rotation, scale and alpha); that path has neither outline clips on children nor shadows. A `SurfaceView` is kept out of the GPU path because it follows its position through a `RenderNode.PositionUpdateListener` on its own node (`SurfaceView.java`, `SurfaceViewPositionUpdateListener`): a second renderer drawing that node would report the offscreen frame's position to the live surface and call `positionLost` when it goes away, moving or hiding the video on screen. With `handleGLSurfaceViewOnAndroid`, each visible `SurfaceView` below the view is then copied with `PixelCopy` over the finished bitmap at its place, as the library does (a hole otherwise); an invalid surface, a refused copy or no answer in 5 s leaves the hole and logs. A bitmap is recycled on every path; a failure rejects `E_UNABLE_TO_SNAPSHOT` and nothing thrown on the main thread escapes.

**Capture, to check on a device** (nothing here ran on one; the unit tests cover the decision of what the engine answers, the file clean-up and the JavaScript path only): in the lab's `ViewShot` row capture (1) a rounded card, `overflow: "hidden"` with a `borderRadius` and an image or a coloured child that reaches the corners: the PNG must have transparent rounded corners and the child clipped to them (a software draw gave square corners); an elevated child (`elevation`) must cast a shadow in the image and not in a software capture; (2) the Rive row, a `TextureView`: the capture must show the animation's frame, not a hole (if the GPU path shows a hole on a device, the texture walk of the software path is the fallback to switch to for trees with a `TextureView`); (3) the video row, a `SurfaceView` by default: with `options={{ handleGLSurfaceViewOnAndroid: true }}` the frame must be in the image at the right place and size, without the option a hole, and in both cases the video on screen must not blink, move or disappear during or after the capture (the reason the tree is drawn in software); (4) `format: "jpg"`, `width` and `height` scaling, `result: "base64"` and `"data-uri"`, and a capture of a view that has just been laid out; (5) a `tmpfile` result exists until `releaseCapture` and the files are gone after the app's process is destroyed (kill the app, list `cache/` for `ReactNative-snapshot-image*`); (6) a capture from a `WebView` row was not considered: a `WebView` in the tree takes the GPU path, which may draw it blank.

**`animatedProps`** (`fabric/animation.ts`) is `animatedStyle` for props that are not style, as `useAnimatedProps` is to `useAnimatedStyle` in Reanimated: a second view-descriptor set and mapper per node, the same ported `styleUpdater` (it gained the `isAnimatedProps` flag the original has) writing with `updateProps(descriptors, values, true)`, which hands the values to Reanimated unbuilt (`stylePropsBuilder` is for style), and the first-run values merged into the node's props, so the first commit shows them and later commits of other props do not resend `text`. Any host element can take it (`native-animated-view`, `native-foreign`). `tests/animation.test.ts` checks the descriptor sets, mappers, the flag and the clean-up with stubs for Reanimated. What works for `AnimateableText` is therefore known up to Reanimated's boundary: the worklet runs on the UI runtime, reads the shared value and writes `{ text }` through `updateProps(..., true)`. What happens after it was not run: Reanimated 4.5.1's `ReanimatedModuleProxy` takes `text` out of the synchronous set (it is not an opacity, transform or colour) and commits a clone of the shadow node with the new prop, which the engine's delegate sees as an `Update` of a foreign root and hands to React Native, whose `ReactTextViewManager` takes the new paragraph state; a device has to confirm the commit rate (the string is laid out natively on each commit, unlike a transform).

**On demand on Android.** `androidOnDemandViewManagers` also lists `RNPDFPackage` (`RNPDFPdfView`), `RiveReactNativePackage` (`RiveReactNativeView`; its two modules stay with the package through the legacy wrapper), `FastShadowPackage` (`FastShadowView`) and `ReanimatedTextPackage` (`JBAnimatedText`); all four are plain `ReactPackage`s, so they take `SvelteNativeOnDemandLegacyPackage`. A test reads the names and the package kinds back from the libraries' sources. react-native-view-shot and react-native-blob-util have no view managers, and expo-conic-gradient is an Expo module outside the expo-modules-core patch's lazy list, so Expo builds it at start like expo-image (`ExpoViews.prepare` leaves it alone).

**Lab.** The same three modes (`/__dev/foreign?mode=libraries`, `libraries-list`, `libraries-sheet`) hold twelve sections: the PDF (`lab-pdf.ts`, a 1.1 KB two-page document), a `ViewShot` of stripes with a Capture button and the image it makes, Rive (a file from Rive's CDN, so it needs the network), a conic gradient, a `ShadowedView` around a `Pressable`, and the animated text counting 0 to 100 on a shared value. `libraryHandles` also hands out the PDF's and Rive's functions, `capture()` and the shared value.

**Verified** (second lane): `bun test` in `packages/svelte-native` (448 tests: nodes, props, payloads, commands and events per library against the fake Fabric binding; blob-util's module faked with a file table; the library's own `captureRef` options, `ShadowedView` corner radii and `shadowStyle` run on the same inputs; static configs against the Flow specs, the Android managers' props and events and the iOS manager's properties), `pnpm typecheck`, `pnpm lint`, `tsc -p tests/tsconfig.json`, the platform app's typecheck, lint and `qa/test`, and the Android release build with all twelve libraries (`native-qa build --abi x86_64`; the C++ of animateable-text and the codegen of react-native-pdf are autolinked and compile; 0 `e:` lines). **Not verified**: anything on a device or an emulator (rendering, touch inside the shadow and the capture rows, the PDF through AndroidPdfViewer, Rive on the CDN's file, `captureView`'s drawing (the list above), the Reanimated commit of `text`), and iOS entirely.

### react-native-svg

Written 28 Oct 2026 (branch `feat/svelte-native-svg`). react-native-svg 15.15.4 (what `npx expo install react-native-svg` gives Expo SDK 57; an app dependency and a native rebuild, like the libraries above) as Svelte components in `@rasheed/svelte-native/svg`: `Svg`, `G`, `Path`, `Rect`, `Circle`, `Ellipse`, `Line`, `Polygon`, `Polyline`, `Text`, `TSpan`, `TextPath`, `Use`, `Image`, `Defs`, `ClipPath`, `LinearGradient`, `RadialGradient`, `Stop`, `Pattern`, `Mask`, `Marker`, `Symbol`, `ForeignObject`, `Filter`, `FeBlend`, `FeColorMatrix`, `FeComposite`, `FeDropShadow`, `FeFlood`, `FeGaussianBlur`, `FeMerge`, `FeMergeNode`, `FeOffset`, and `SvgXml`, `SvgUri`. The props, defaults and style handling are the library's: each element sends the Fabric node its class sends for the same props (`tests/svg-elements.test.ts` mounts the same tree through the library's own element classes and through the components, on both platforms, and compares view names, props and children: 146 comparisons).

**What is the library's own.** The prop extraction is imported by deep path from `react-native-svg/src/lib` (`svg-library.js`, with a hand-written `.d.ts` so the type checker never compiles the library's TypeScript): `extractProps` (and through it `extractFill`, `extractStroke`, `extractBrush`, `extractResponder`, `extractOpacity`, `extractLengthList`), `extractTransform`, `extractViewBox`, `extractPolyPoints`, `units`, `maskType`, `util`. None imports React; their `react-native` import is `processColor` and `PanResponder`, which Metro gives the lite shim (`LITE_ORIGINS` in `metro.ts` lists `react-native-svg`; the shim exports `PanResponder`, loaded when the library's `extractResponder` reads its handler names). `tests/svg-bundle.test.ts` bundles the entry the way Metro does and fails on React in any form, on any file of the library besides those 17, and on any React Native import that is not a deep path; the release bundle of the app (source map of `native-qa build`) has the same 17 files. Ported because they are written against React children: `extractText`'s `extractFont` and font string parser, `extractGradient`, the `extractFilter` functions, `xml.tsx`'s parser and `fetchText` (`foreign-svg-extract.ts`, `foreign-svg-xml.ts`), compared with the library's own functions on the same inputs (the parser's tree for the six icons below is equal to the library's).

**The Android tree.** `Svg` mounts `native-foreign` with the component `RNSVGSvgViewAndroid` (iOS: `RNSVGSvgView`), the library's `G` inside it, and every element below is a `native-foreign` node of its own (`RNSVGPath`, `RNSVGText`, ...). The host registers each name with the engine before the first commit. The engine's delegate then sees the `Svg` as a foreign root (kind 19, the `EngineForeign` frame at the layout Yoga gave the `Svg`) and everything under it as descendants: every mutation goes to React Native's own mounting unchanged. React Native creates the `SvgView` (a `ReactViewGroup`) and the shapes (`RenderableView`s, `ViewGroup`s the `SvgView` draws itself; `SvgViewManager.needsCustomLayoutForChildren`) with the library's view managers, lays them out and sends them their props and commands; the engine keeps the frame's place, transform, opacity, clip and stacking, as for a map and its markers. The library's TurboModules find a view by its tag (`SvgViewManager.getSvgViewByTag`, `RenderableViewManager`), which is the Fabric node's tag the host handle gives, so the refs below work. A `ForeignObject`'s children (`View`s, `Text`) are descendants too: they draw and lay out, and take no JavaScript touch. The 29 view managers are created on first use (`com.horcrux.svg.SvgPackage` in `androidOnDemandViewManagers`; the package is a `BaseReactPackage`, so the wrapper serves its two TurboModules, `RNSVGRenderableModule` and `RNSVGSvgViewModule`, as before). A test reads the 29 names from `RenderableViewManager.java` and `SvgViewManager.java`. **iOS**: the engine does not replace React Native's mounting there, so the same `native-foreign` nodes are ordinary Fabric components (`RNSVGSvgView` and the rest); `host.ts` and the view configs are shared. Nothing was compiled or run on iOS.

**View configs** (`foreign-svg-kinds.ts`, registered when the entry loads: `defineViewKind` for each name): the attributes and events of the 30 codegen specs of the library (29 on Android: iOS has its own root), `src` as an asset source and `color` as a colour. `tests/svg-configs.test.ts` runs the library's spec files through the Babel codegen plugin Metro uses and compares each generated config with ours: the same attributes, the same converters (on colour and asset samples) and events, on both platforms (iOS's generated config also lists each event handler as an attribute, which no native view reads and the tables leave out), and the payload the host builds from the props of every element of the test set (more than 100 nodes) equals the one React Native's own payload code builds.

**Text.** A string between the tags of a `Text` or `TextPath` is a `TSpan` of its own, and the only string in a `TSpan` is its `content`: the library works that out from its React children, a Svelte component sees none, so the host does it when it builds the node (`fabric/foreign-text.ts`: the library tells the host which Fabric names are text containers and spans, and what a span made of a string sends; the host reads the string children of the Svelte node: a lone string is the `content`, any other string is a span, the empty strings Svelte leaves where a block starts and comments are neither). A text change is a clone of the span with the new `content`. Differences from JSX that follow from Svelte's markup: `Hello {name}!` is one string (JSX makes three), whitespace between two elements is a string (as in HTML), and `SvgXml`'s `tspan` and `textPath` take their children as an array as the library's XML renderer gives them (`childrenAsArray`, internal), which makes a span of a lone string instead of its `content`, so the nodes are the library's.

**Gradients, merges.** The library reads a `Stop`'s props from its parent's React children; here a `Stop` (and an `FeMergeNode`) registers a getter for its props with its parent through context, and the parent reads them when it builds its props, so a stop that changes, comes or goes updates the gradient (a changed list is a clone of the gradient node) and the first commit already has the stops. Stops draw nothing and have no native view, but each renders a `<native-anchor>` (an element the host builds no view for; it only serves its `ref`), whose handle has `position()`: the index among the siblings at each level from the root. The gradient and the merge read their members in the order of those positions (`components/svg/registry.ts`), so a stop or a merge node that arrives later than the first render (an `{#if}` turning on between two others) lands at its place in the document and not last; the sort by offset keeps that order for equal offsets, and a merge's layers are the document's. The position is known once the host has built the anchor, so a late member is read last for one flush and the parent reads again when the order differs. Members that are moved without being created again (a keyed `{#each}` reordering them) are not noticed until the parent reads again for another reason.

**`SvgXml` and `SvgUri`.** `SvgXml xml={...}` parses with the port of the library's parser and draws the tree with the components (`SvgNode.svelte` maps the tag names of `xmlTags.ts`; a tag the library does not know draws nothing, with its children, and the filter elements without a native view warn once, as in the library); the props of the component replace the root element's. `onError` and a `fallback` snippet as in the library; `SvgUri uri` fetches (a network or file URI, or an SVG data URI: `utf8` on Android, `base64` on both) and shows `SvgXml`, with `onLoad`. `tests/svg-xml.test.ts` compares six documents (`tests/svg-icons.ts`: an icon of paths, gradients with a clip path, text with spans, symbols drawn by `use`, transforms with a view box scaled by `slice`, a mask) with what the library's parser and element classes mount for them, on both platforms, plus the parser's tree against the library's, CDATA, comments, unquoted values, a `style` attribute and unknown elements, and the library's error message for a bad document.

**Refs** hand the host handle (`tag`, `measure`, `measureInWindow`, `dispatchCommand`) the library's methods: `getBBox(options)`, `getTotalLength()`, `getPointAtLength(length)`, `isPointInFill({ x, y })`, `isPointInStroke`, `getCTM`, `getScreenCTM` call `RNSVGRenderableModule` with the view's tag as `Shape.tsx` does; `setNativeProps` converts brushes (and a transform on `G`, `Text`, `TSpan`, `TextPath`) before it goes through Fabric; `Svg`'s handle adds `toDataURL(callback, options)` (`RNSVGSvgViewModule`). Results are plain objects (`{ a, b, c, d, e, f }`, `{ x, y }`), not the library's `SVGMatrix` and `SVGPoint` classes. The module calls are tested with fake TurboModules and the tag the handle carries (`tests/svg-native.test.ts`); they were not run against the native modules.

**Touch.** `onPress`, `onPressIn`, `onPressOut`, `onLongPress`, `delayPressIn`, `delayPressOut`, `delayLongPress`, `disabled`, `pressRetentionOffset` and the responder handlers of the library's `extractResponder` (`onStartShouldSetResponder`, `onResponderGrant`, ...) are taken as the library takes them: `responsible: true` and the handlers the caller gave go to the native view (`responsible` is what the native hit test looks for), and the press handlers run through the host's Pressability and responder system, which a touch event for the node reaches (tested by sending `topTouchStart` and `topTouchEnd` to a shape's node). The six `touchableHandle*` flags the library's Touchable mixin adds next to `responsible` are not sent (the mixin is not here). **What the engine does on Android**: a touch that lands in the frame of a foreign root is delivered to the view by the platform and never reaches JavaScript's touch dispatch ("Touch gating"; the target is the `SvgView`, which has no engine tag), so a shape's `onPress` does not run on Android. `pointerEvents="box-only"` on the `Svg` makes the engine keep the touches of the frame, which JavaScript then hears on the `Svg`'s own tag: the `Svg`'s `onPress` and responder handlers work (the lab's gradient card does this), a shape inside it is not a target. A hit test of the shapes in JavaScript (`isPointInFill` of each `responsible` shape for the point of the root's touch, front to back) would give shapes their press on Android; it is not built. On iOS the touch path is React Native's, which asks the native views (`responsible`) for the target, so a shape's `onPress` is expected to work there; not run.

**Animation.** `animatedProps` on any element is a worklet returning native props, as `useAnimatedProps` does: `{ r: radius.value, cx: x.value }` for a `Circle`, `{ d: path.value }` for a `Path`, `{ strokeDashoffset }`, `{ opacity }`, `{ matrix: [...] }`; a colour (`fill`, `stroke`, `floodColor`) is a CSS string (`"#ff8000"`, `"rgba(0, 128, 255, 0.5)"`, a name) or a number in Reanimated's order, `0xRRGGBBAA`, and not the `{ type: 0, payload }` brush the library's JavaScript builds: the UI thread writes the values without that JavaScript, and Reanimated 4.5.1's `updateProps` runs `processColorsInProps` on animated props (`updateProps.ts`, `isAnimatedProps`), which turns a string or an RGBA number into the platform's integer (a signed ARGB `int` on Android, unsigned on iOS: React Native's `processColor`, compared with it on eight colours on both platforms in `tests/svg-colors.test.ts`, the real function bundled) and throws "Invalid color value" for an object. The native views take that integer: Android's `RenderableView.setFill(Dynamic)`, `setStroke(Dynamic)` and `FeFloodView.setFloodColor(Dynamic)` read a number as `[0, asInt()]` and iOS's `RNSVGBrush:` makes a solid colour of an `NSNumber` (the Fabric props are `UnsafeMixed<ColorValue | ColorStruct>`; a test reads the library's sources for those branches). `animation.ts` processes the first values of an `animatedProps` worklet the same way, so the first commit has the integer too. The first values are in the first commit and the key never reaches the payload; a node without `animatedProps` does not load the animation layer. Tested up to Reanimated's boundary against the same stubs as `animatedProps` of the animated text: a `Circle`'s `r` and `cx` and a `Path`'s `d` driven from shared values (`tests/svg-native.test.ts`: the first values, one descriptor set and one mapper per node, the mapper's write is `updateProps(..., true)` with the changed values, everything stopped with the node). What Reanimated 4.5.1 does with them is the commit path described for `AnimateableText`: these props are not opacity, transform or colour, so each change is a commit of a clone of the shadow node on the UI thread, which the engine's delegate hands to React Native as an `Update` of a foreign descendant, and the native view parses the new `r` or `d` on that commit; the rate a path `d` can be animated at was not measured. The lab animates a ring's `strokeDashoffset` and a dot's `r` from one shared value.

**Not ported:** `SvgCss`, `SvgCssUri`, `SvgWithCss`, `SvgWithCssUri`, `LocalSvg`, `WithLocalSvg`, `inlineStyles` and `parse`'s `middleware` (the style-sheet inliner of `css/css.tsx` needs css-tree and css-select and the library's React components), `SvgFromXml`, `SvgFromUri` and `SvgAst` (the class and element forms of `SvgXml`), `FilterImage`, the filter elements the library itself leaves to the web (`FeComponentTransfer`, `FeConvolveMatrix`, `FeDiffuseLighting`, `FeDisplacementMap`, `FeDistantLight`, `FeImage`, `FeMorphology`, `FePointLight`, `FeSpecularLighting`, `FeSpotLight`, `FeTile`, `FeTurbulence`, `FeFunc*`: it warns and draws nothing on native; `SvgXml` does the same), the `SVGMatrix` and `SVGPoint` classes, the Touchable mixin's responder flags, `hitSlop` on shapes (the `Svg` root takes it), the web build (`elements.web`), Windows.

**Lab.** The thirteenth section of `/__dev/foreign?mode=libraries` (also `libraries-list`, one row, and `libraries-sheet`): two static icons of `Path`s, a gradient card (`Defs`, `LinearGradient`, `Stop`, `Rect`, `Text`; its `Svg` takes touches and reports `svg-press`), a progress ring and dot animated from one shared value, and `SvgXml` of the six icons (`lab-svg.ts`). `libraryHandles` gives the first icon's `Path` handle and the card's `Svg` handle (`getTotalLength`, `getBBox`, `toDataURL`, ...) and the shared value (`ring`). The route stays under `notCovered` in `qa/lib/screens.ts`, with the reason extended: the ring moves, so no frame of the screen is stable, and the rasterised icons are the library's own rendering, which a reference image would pin to one device; a separate static screen would need reference images from a device run.

**Verified**: `bun test` in `packages/svelte-native` (698 tests after the review fixes of 31 Oct, among them `tests/refs.test.ts` (the ref contract), `tests/svg-colors.test.ts` (Reanimated's real `processColorsInProps`), `tests/foreign-shadow-geometry.test.ts` (React Native's Yoga compiled for the host) and `src/test/run.sh`'s `CaptureCheck.kt` and `CaptureFilesCheck.kt` for the capture, and `tests/svg-elements.test.ts` (the elements against the library's classes), `svg-configs.test.ts`, `svg-xml.test.ts`, `svg-extract.test.ts` (the ported extractors against the library's functions), `svg-dynamic.test.ts`, `svg-native.test.ts` (refs, touch, animated props), `svg-bundle.test.ts`, the on-demand names in `foreign.test.ts`, the lite shim's `PanResponder`, and the lowering test below; the oracle is `tests/svg-oracle.ts`, the library's own sources bundled with React), `pnpm typecheck`, `pnpm lint`, `tsc -p tests/tsconfig.json`, the platform app's typecheck, eslint and `qa/test`, and the Android release build with react-native-svg linked (`native-qa build --abi x86_64`: the codegen and native sources compile, the Metro bundle holds the 17 library files and none of React's elements, 0 `e:` lines). **Not verified**: anything on a device or an emulator (rendering, the `SvgView` inside the engine's frame, the TurboModule calls, touch, scrolling in a row or a sheet, animation rate), and iOS entirely. **Found on the way**: `lower.ts` rewrote every `Text` and `Image` imported from any entry of the package into the primitives' host tags, which would have turned the SVG `Text` and `Image` into `native-text` and `native-image`; the svg entry is now excluded (`tests/lower.test.ts`).

### Device verification of the libraries

Written 1 Nov 2026 (branch `feat/svelte-native-wp2dev`). `native-qa foreign --device <serial>` (`apps/platform/qa/README.md`, "Foreign") checks the thirteen native-view libraries on a device, and three gates hold for each: `foreign.<library>.failedChecks <= 0`, `foreign.<library>.checks >=` the count a clean run makes (so a check that quietly stops running fails), and `foreign.<library>.skipped <= 0`. It runs the lab twice, `run=foreign-libraries,drive:adb` and then `run=foreign-leftovers`, and reads the release bundle's source map. The scenario mounts each library in the three places the lab has (inline in a `ScrollView`, in a row of the windowed `List` with its neighbours mounted too, in a native sheet) and runs `src/lib/native/dev/foreign-libraries-checks.ts` there; every check is a line of its own in logcat (`[Svelte foreign library]`), and one a platform cannot make is `[Svelte foreign library skipped]`, reported and never counted as a pass (on Android every check can be made, so a skip fails its gate). Hermes writes a logged string to logcat with `'` as `\'` and `\` as `\\`; the parser undoes that before it reads the JSON, and a tagged line that is still not a check (cut, not JSON, an unknown place) is `foreign.unparsedLines`, gated at 0, and printed.

**Read from the platform, not from JavaScript.** `SvelteEngine.inspect(tag)` (`Inspect.kt`) answers what Android built for a tag: the class, its size and screen position in pixels, attached, shown, the text it holds, the classes of the views inside it (with their size and how each `ViewGroup` clips: `clip=canvas` for an engine view with `overflow: hidden`, `clip=children` for a `clipChildren` group, `clip=none`), the surfaces among them, and how the parent clips. It finds a foreign view and everything inside one through React Native's mounting manager, and an engine view through the engine's registry. `SvelteQaPixels.sample(source, points, radius)` (QA module `svelte-qa-android`, QA variant only) decodes a `captureRef` result (file, data URI or base64) and answers the mean ARGB of the square around each point (fractions of the image) and the MIME type the decoder found; the colour a prop gave is compared with what the platform drew. iOS has the same two as `SvelteQA.inspect` and `SvelteQA.samplePixels` (`SvelteQAViews.swift`, `inspect` reports `clip` as `masksToBounds` or `none` for the view and its parent), written to the same shapes and not compiled or run. `inspect` returns the text of any view, a password field's included, `accessibilityNodes` what a service would read of the tree, and `tap`, `drag`, `performAccessibilityAction` and `profileJs` press the screen, perform an action and write to the file system: all six refuse outside the QA package (`requireLab`, `LabSwitches.allowed`), as `SvelteQaPixels` is a module of the QA variant only; `qa/test/product-modules.test.ts` asserts the module lists and scans the engine module for every function, which is either named there as the product's or guarded. A section's props are `libraryLab` (`foreign.svelte.ts`): the check changes one, the section's component gets the new prop as any prop update, and the check reads what the native view did. The press of a native dialog's button is the harness's (`foreign-press.ts`): the lab logs `[Svelte foreign press]` with a resource id (when it was opened with `drive:adb`), the harness dumps the UI tree and taps it with `adb input`.

**The checks** (each in each place): *mounts* (a view of the library's class is attached, shown, sized, and its size is the layout the engine measured: the class patterns are the libraries' own, read from their sources. The view sized is the library's own, never the lab's bordered box around the section: the measured handle's own view, or the pinned view below the inspected one (`foreign-layout.ts`, tested in `qa/test/foreign-layout.test.ts`); on iOS `ShadowedView` is a plain `View`, so its card is inspected, and a pin that is not in the tree is a failed check, not a comparison with the box), *prop update* (the colour of a capture after the prop changed), *events* (the callback ran, with its payload), *commands* (the result or the effect), and *leaving drops every frame and node* (`foreignStats`). Per library:

| Library | What is read |
|---|---|
| expo-image | `ExpoImageView`; `onLoad`, `onDisplay`, no `onError`; `source` red, then blue: a capture shows each |
| expo-blur | `ExpoBlurView`; the two sides of a stripe edge inside the blur differ by 130 at intensity 1 and 5 at intensity 100, outside it by 140 |
| expo-linear-gradient | the native gradient's colours at both corners, then new colours (the list keeps its length: the Android prop setter ignores a removed `locations`) |
| react-native-video | `ReactExoplayerView` and its surface; `onLoadStart`, `onLoad`, `onReadyForDisplay`, `onProgress`; `paused` and `pause()`/`resume()` stop and restart the progress; `seek` raises `onSeek`, `getCurrentPosition`; a capture without `handleGLSurfaceViewOnAndroid` is a hole, with it the frame is there, and the player does not move or stop while it is drawn |
| datetimepicker | Android: the dialog's own `dismiss` finds the dialog `open` made, the pending promise ends with null; with `drive:adb` the harness presses OK (a date), Cancel (null) and the time dialog's OK. iOS: the view mounts; a pick needs a finger |
| lottie-react-native | `onAnimationLoaded`; `progress` moves the dot (0 and 0.5 differ at 24 units from the middle); `play(0, 60)` ends with `onAnimationFinish(false)`; `pause()` holds an animation, `resume()` lets it end |
| react-native-pdf | `PdfView`; `onLoadComplete(2)`, `onPageChanged(1/2)`; `page` 2 and `setPage(1)` raise `onPageChanged` and the capture shows the page's colour |
| react-native-view-shot | the rounded card (`overflow: hidden`) has transparent corners and its stripes inside, an elevated child casts its shadow in the image, `jpg`, `width`/`height`, `base64` and `data-uri` results decode to what they claim, `ViewShot.capture()` and `onCapture`, `releaseCapture` deletes the file; the next launch finds none of the files this one left |
| rive-react-native | `RiveReactNativeView`; `onPlay`; a capture shows the animation (not a hole); `fit` fill paints the sides contain leaves bare; `pause()`/`play()` raise `onPause`/`onPlay` (the file is Rive's CDN's: the lab needs the network) |
| expo-conic-gradient | hard stops: the right half red and the left blue, and `angle` 180 swaps them |
| react-native-fast-shadow | `FastShadowView`; two cards in the rounded `overflow: hidden` box, one a direct child of it (the views between are flattened) and one in an unflattened `collapsable={false}` wrapper with an `overflow: hidden` of its own; both boxes clip their canvas (`clip=canvas`), the shadow is drawn below both cards outside their frames, `shadowOpacity` 0 takes both away, a press on the child reaches JavaScript |
| react-native-animateable-text | the native string follows the shared value, advances while the JavaScript thread is blocked for 900 ms, and a plain `text` prop changes it |
| react-native-svg | `SvgView`; `getTotalLength`, `getBBox`, `toDataURL` answer; the heart is its fill and a new fill repaints it; the card's gradient runs orange to teal; the animated ring changes between captures; a press on the `Svg` reaches `onPress` |

**What the first runs found, and what was done** (OVH emulator, x86_64 release build, 1 Nov 2026). Wrapper and engine failures, each fixed with a test where the host can run one:

1. *The animation layer swallowed a foreign view's own props.* Once the layer was loaded (any animated node earlier in the app), every top-level prop starting `animation` or `transition` was taken as a Reanimated CSS key and removed from the payload: expo-image's `transition` (a number) reached the CSS parser, which threw `iterator method is not callable`, a JavaScript fatal that destroyed the React host; Rive's `animationName` never reached its view. A top-level prop is a CSS key only when the node's view config does not declare it as an attribute (`AnimationTarget.declares`, from `config.attributes`): `transition`, `animationName` and `<Modal animationType>` are the views' own and reach them, while an engine node nested in a foreign view (no config entry for `animation*`) keeps its CSS (`tests/foreign-animation.test.ts`).
2. *react-native-video never mounted.* Fabric names a component by its React view name without the `RCT` prefix (`componentNameByReactViewName`: `RCTVideo` is `Video`) and a shadow view says that name; the registered `RCTVideo` never matched, the node became an engine view with no children, and no event, command or frame of the player existed. `registerForeignComponent` and `isForeignName` call React Native's own `componentNameByReactViewName` (the host test compiles its source and asserts that both use the Fabric name), not a copy of its rule (`foreign::Names`, `ForeignSplitTest.cpp`).
3. *A foreign view's shadow was cut off at its frame.* `overflow: hidden` on Android was `clipChildren`, which cuts each child off at its own bounds; react-native-fast-shadow draws outside its bounds, and its frame was a direct child of the lab's rounded `overflow: hidden` box (layout-only views between them are flattened): the card showed the shadow in its corners only. Every engine view with `overflow: hidden` now clips its canvas to its padding box (its bounds inset by its border), as `ReactViewGroup`'s `clipToPaddingBox` does, and keeps `clipChildren` off (`EngineView.hidesOverflow`). It is not decided by whether a foreign frame is below it: a count of foreign descendants would flip a sibling's clipping when a foreign child mounts, and the first version (direct parent only) left a card in an unflattened wrapper cut off at its own bounds. The lab's shadow section has both cards, and `parity --quick` is unchanged by it. A rounded `overflow: hidden` view still clips to its outline (`RoundedOutline`) as before; the cost is a `clipRect` on the canvas per such view, to be measured on the A16 (`list-300-clipped`).
4. *A frame with `pointerEvents` box-only never heard its touches in JavaScript* (`dispatchTouch: no event emitter for tag`): the engine kept no emitter for a foreign root, so a press on the `Svg` (`pointerEvents="box-only"`) was dropped. The engine keeps the root's emitter now (`MountingDelegate::setEmitter` for roots).
5. *tmpfile captures outlived the process that made them.* `OnDestroy` does not run in a killed process (recents swipe, low-memory kill, `am force-stop`), and a `tmpfile` result lasts "until the app exits", so the files stayed in the cache directory for good. A new process sweeps the library's `ReactNative-snapshot-image*` files older than itself in `cacheDir` and in `externalCacheDir` (view-shot writes to either) when the engine module is created (`CaptureFiles.sweep`, `CaptureFilesCheck.kt`); `foreign-leftovers` is the check, in the launch after the one that made them: it lists both directories, expects the six files the first launch made (`expect:6`) and fails on a listing it cannot read.
6. *Escaped log lines were dropped.* The first harness took a line for a check only when its quoted JSON parsed as it stood; Hermes escapes `'` and `\` in the logcat text, so every check whose name or detail held an apostrophe or a JSON `\"` (102 of 438 lines) was silently missing from the counts, and three failing "leaving drops every frame and node" checks (shadow, text and svg in a row: `frames: 9, nodes: 14`) among them. The parser unescapes, a tagged line it still cannot read fails the run (`foreign.unparsedLines`), the fixture is a real escaped log line, and the failures those checks showed were the lab's, not the engine's: it read the counts while the page was still leaving, so it now polls for the baseline (frames, nodes and registry back to where the section began) before it judges, and a leak that stays still fails.

Not wrapper failures, and now in the lab as the libraries' own rules: react-native-video 6.19.3 takes `http`, `https`, `content`, `file`, `rtsp` and `asset` URIs on Android and looks any other up as a resource name (`Source.isValidScheme`), so a `data:` URI gives an empty source (the lab's video is a file in the cache directory, `labVideoFile`); expo-linear-gradient's Android prop setter ignores a removed `locations` (`locations?.let`), so a list of colours must keep the length of the list it had; Rive's `vehicles.riv` has no default ViewModel and raises `DataBindingError` through `onError` with the library's own default `dataBinding` (the events and the capture are unaffected); a `SurfaceView` (the default `viewType` of react-native-video) is not clipped to a rounded ancestor's outline, it is composed by the system (`viewType={0}` is the `TextureView` that is).

**React stays out.** The harness's own check reads the build's source map: none of a library's own JavaScript (its React components and wrappers: `expo-image/src`, `react-native-video`, `lottie-react-native`, `react-native-pdf` with `react-native-blob-util`, `react-native-view-shot`, `rive-react-native`, `react-native-fast-shadow`, `react-native-animateable-text`, `expo-blur`, `expo-linear-gradient`, `expo-conic-gradient`, the datetimepicker, and every file of `react-native-svg` outside `src/lib`) may be in the release bundle (`libraryModules` in `qa/lib/foreign.ts`); the files of `react` itself that the app's other dependencies (Reanimated, expo-modules-core) bring are reported as `foreign.react.files`, not gated.

**Result on the OVH emulator** (`localhost:5555`, Android 16, x86_64 release build, software GL; `qa/runs/foreign-localhost_5555-20261101T155513Z.json`, the merge with `feat/svelte-native-next`, QA build `97d0bbcd8838`): `native-qa foreign` gives **450 checks, 0 failed, 0 skipped, 0 unreadable lines** over the thirteen libraries and the three places (image 36, blur 21, gradient 21, video 63, datetime 27, lottie 33, pdf 39, capture 51, rive 33, conic 21, shadow 36, text 33, svg 36; each count is its gate's minimum), both launches complete (276 s and 13 s), no crash, no JavaScript error, no engine warning, `foreign-leftovers` clean, and the release bundle holds none of the libraries' own JavaScript. Every `foreign.<library>.failedChecks`, `.checks` and `.skipped` gate, `foreign.unparsedLines` and the five health gates are pass. (The first full run read 336 of the checks: 102 of its 438 lines were dropped by the parser before the logcat escaping was undone, see finding 6; the Samsung A16 run of the same build is the coordinator's.) Another agent's app launches on the shared emulator now and then (`dev.alfayez.odinsokol` from `adb shell am start`): a launch it interrupted is incomplete by the harness's own rule (`interrupted`), so the command launches again (`--attempts`, three by default) and records how many it took. The lab's own counts of what a launch needs: the first launch of the lab takes 5 min on this emulator (the thirteen libraries in the thirty-nine places, the video, Rive, PDF and the date dialogs with the longest waits).

The existing `foreign-views` scenario (WebView, map, expo-video: 38 checks in "Verification on the OVH emulator") passes with the same build (`lab --run foreign-views`, 99 s on the merged tree).

**Parity of the padding-box clip** (`parity --quick`, OVH emulator, the merged tree, run `parity-localhost_5555-20261101T160206Z`: 81 pass, 17 fail, 4 unstable, 64 signed-in cases skipped for want of the QA account; the same suite before the merge with the old clip semantics: 101 of 102 pass, the one miss `screens-modal`, the first case of the run, not a screen the clip touches). What changed, and why it is the intended change (clip semantics of React Native's `ReactViewGroup`: children are not clipped at their own bounds, an `overflow: hidden` view clips its canvas to the padding box, inside its border):
- `screens-slots` and `screens-toolbar-center`, every quick variant (411, 181 and, large, 709 and 283 px): the segmented control's selected segment used to be painted over the pill's border (a child of a `clipChildren` parent was cut at its own bounds, not at the parent's padding box); the border now stays on top. The change is the border's two pixel rows under the selected segment (diff images in the run's directory).
- `light-rtl-normal` of `dev-rows`, `dev-async`, `dev-async-loading`, `dev-async-error`, `screens-translucent`, `screens-tabs`, `tree-home`, `tree-detail`, `screens-sheet`: 1 to 8 px each, a one-pixel-wide column at the edge of a glyph (x = 1004, 721, 649 and 586): a glyph's overhang is no longer cut at its text view's bounds.
- `login` in all four quick variants is unstable: the merged QA app stays on its "Rasheed" loading screen (the log has `Connecting to SpacetimeDB WS...` and nothing after) so the screen keeps changing. That is the merge with `feat/svelte-native-next`, not the clip: the same cases passed on the unmerged tree; whether the emulator reaches the development database is for the next branch to say.
- Not run: the same cases on the merged tree with the old clip semantics (the emulator was held by other runs for over 25 minutes), which would settle the rtl columns beyond doubt; and the references are not updated: `parity --update --filter screens-slots` (and the other ten screens, all variants) takes the new ones once the change is accepted.

**Open items.** (1) expo-image, the row, on the Samsung A16: "prop update: the native view shows the red source" read a transparent capture at all three points 300 ms after `onLoad` (the first run on the phone; inline and sheet passed, and the emulator passes in all three places). The check now waits for `onDisplay` of the new source and captures again every 400 ms for up to 5 s; a miss records the view's class, size, shown, alpha and the load, display and error counts, which tell a view that never drew (a real bug in the row) from a late one. Confirmed on the emulator, not yet on the phone. (2) A16 `list-300-clipped` and the other clipped lists have not been measured with the clip on every `overflow: hidden` view (one `clipRect` per view and frame): the phone is the check. (3) iOS: nothing here ran there.

**What needs the iPhone** (nothing here ran there; the host and the view configs are shared): compiling and running `SvelteQAViews.swift` (`SvelteQA.inspect`, `SvelteQA.samplePixels`: until they answer, the lab's checks that read the platform are `skipped` there, and the ones that read events, commands and layout run); the iOS class names the mounts check pins (`mounts: it is the library's view` is skipped on iOS and its detail records the class the iPhone built, for the first run to pin); the picker's own `onValueChange` and `onDismiss` (a finger on `UIDatePicker`); the video's `AVPlayerLayer` (no surface, no hole: the capture checks assume nothing of it); `captureRef` of Rive's and Lottie's iOS views; a `bar` for `ios` on each `foreign.*` gate and `foreign` in the iPhone's `runs` (`qa/gates.json`), and `native-qa foreign --platform ios` (it refuses today with a message, as the iOS lab is launched through `devicectl`).

### Limits

- A foreign view lives in the frame's rectangle: a child that draws outside it (a map callout, a WebView's full-screen video) is the library's own window or overlay, not part of the engine's tree.
- `react-native-maps` tiles need a Google Maps API key in the manifest (`GOOGLE_MAPS_API_KEY`); the QA variant carries a placeholder, which mounts the map, makes it ready and loads no tiles.
- Not ported from the React components: the WebView's `renderLoading`/`renderError`/`startInLoadingState`/`nativeConfig`, the map's event payload conversions, `AnimatedRegion`, the React components of expo-video (`VideoView`'s methods: `enterFullscreen`, `startPictureInPicture`, are on the native view's prototype and reachable through the module, not through the handle).
- Each `MapView` the Google Maps SDK has shown stays reachable after its node is deleted (about 40 `View` objects; heap paths in "Verification on the OVH emulator"); the engine's and React Native's registries are clean, and WebViews and video views are released. An app that mounts maps without end grows by that amount.
- iOS: foreign views already mount through React Native's iOS mounting, which the engine does not replace there; `host.ts` and the view configs apply, nothing was compiled or run on iOS.
- A foreign root that is moved in the engine tree keeps its view (the second `Insert` is not forwarded); a root reparented across surfaces is not supported.
- Foreign views and engine overlays: a foreign view in a modal layer or a sheet works the same way (the layer is an engine view); a `SurfaceView` (video, map) is composed by the system and follows the platform's z-order inside the window, not the engine's `zIndex` against another `SurfaceView`.

### Verification on the OVH emulator

`node qa/native-qa.ts lab --device localhost:5555 --apk <apk> --run foreign-views --timeout 420` runs the scenario `foreign-views` (not part of `quick`; the page is `/__dev/foreign`, driven by `src/lib/native/dev/foreign-lab.ts`). It passes **38 of 38 checks** (run `qa/runs/lab-localhost_5555-20261026T151031Z.json`):

- **Inline**: the WebView loads its page and posts `ready:v1` through the messaging module, `onLoadEnd` arrives, a prop update (the source) reloads it (`ready:v2`), `injectJavaScript` runs in the page; the map becomes ready, `setCamera` moves the native camera and `getCamera` reads it back through `RNMapsAirModule`; the video renders its first frame and plays; the engine counts three frames, none waiting, four nodes owned by React Native. Touch: a tap on the WebView reaches the page and not JavaScript's touch dispatch; a tap on the video reaches the view (which reports it itself as a touch event of its own tag); a pan on the map reaches react-native-maps' view (`onPanDrag`) and neither it nor a tap on the map reaches JavaScript's dispatch; a tap on a plain `Pressable` beside them still does. Leaving the page returns frames, nodes and the registry to where they were.
- **List**: a windowed `List` of 24 rows of foreign views (WebView, map, video in turn): the first rows load, a tap in a row reaches its page, scrolling to the last row unmounts the first rows' views and scrolling back mounts fresh ones that load again; leaving drops everything.
- **Sheet**: a native sheet with the three views: they load, a tap reaches the page, closing drops everything.
- **Cycles**: 20 mounts and unmounts of each kind, one after the other: frames 0 to 0, nodes 0 to 0, registry leaks 0, 20 created and 20 deleted, the engine's own view count unchanged (99 to 99), all 20 ready; the WebView and video view objects are released after a collection (reachable 13 to 13 and 33 to 33).
- **End**: the router is back where it began and the engine and React Native hold no frame, node or registry entry.

`dumpsys meminfo` (`Views`, after each kind's 20 cycles, without a forced collection; `scratchpad/leak.sh`): 720 before the cycles, 797 after the WebViews (`WebViews` 3 to 0), 1658 after the maps, 1654 after the videos, 2386 after the maps without markers. `ViewRootImpl` 1 and `Activities` 1 throughout.

Frames, same run: p50 17 ms, p95 79 ms, 18.6 % dropped over 81.6 s: the scenario creates WebViews, a map renderer and a media codec in turn on a software-GPU emulator, so it carries no frame gate.

**Reported, not judged** (the scenario's `detail.observed`): on this emulator the Google map never moves its camera for a finger, never reports `onPress` for a tap, and never raises `onRegionChangeComplete` after a camera animation (`regionEvents` 0; the camera that `getCamera` returns after `animateToRegion` is unchanged). The placeholder Maps key fails authorization (`Authorization failure` in the log) and the legacy renderer of the emulator's Play services draws no tiles, so everything that needs the renderer's frames is unobserved here; the touch reaches the map view (`onPanDrag`, the hit-test dump `hit MapView, [MapView [FrameLayout [as ...]]]`) and real `adb input tap` events behave the same as the lab's. Run the scenario on a device with a real key to see the three observed values change.

**Map views stay reachable** (`observed` entry `cycle map view objects still reachable after a collection`, 13 to 33 after 20 cycles, and 33 to 53 for a map without its marker): the Google Maps SDK keeps every MapView it has shown. A heap dump taken after the cycles (`Debug.dumpHprofData`, Shark's shortest paths to all `com.rnmaps.maps.MapView` instances: 53 instances, 2 distinct paths) goes in both cases through Google classes only: (1) a thread (the map's vector renderer, `gmm6.vector.n`/`p`) to its GL view `gmm6.api.as`, whose parents are a `FrameLayout` and the `MapView`; (2) the window's `ViewTreeObserver.mOnGlobalLayoutListeners`, which holds `lib6.impl.cy` (the logo's layout listener, never removed), then the logo `ImageView`, `RelativeLayout`, `FrameLayout`, `as`, `internal.ai`/`dn`/`zzz` and react-native-maps' `MapView$7` (its `OnMapReadyCallback`) to the `MapView`. No path goes through the engine (frames, `EngineForeign`, `ForeignViews`, the registry), React Native's mounting manager or a view of ours; WebViews and videos, which take the same route through the engine, are released. About 40 `View` objects stay per map. Without a stock React Native control run this is the SDK's behavior as the heap shows it, not a measured difference between hosts.

**Pop of a screen with foreign views**: a screen recording of `router.back()` on the inline page (`scratchpad/popvideo.sh`) shows the WebView, the map and the video still drawn inside the sliding screen for the length of its exit.

**Not verified**: the Samsung A16, the iPhone and iOS in general (the host and the view configs are shared, nothing of this was compiled or run there), map tiles and the Google map's own gestures and events (need a Maps key), TalkBack on a foreign view, a WebView with a file or `content://` source, expo-video's fullscreen and picture-in-picture (`setPictureInPictureParams: Current activity does not support picture-in-picture` is logged by the system when the player is released, the activity does not declare it), `AnimatedRegion`-style map animation from JavaScript.

## Native modules (smoke test)

WP3 of the Svelte Native program: the native libraries of the production app (`~/Documents/git/mobile`, Expo SDK 57 like this one) load, and each answers one core call, under Svelte Native (Svelte 5 on React Native Fabric without React; Android on this engine, iOS on React Native's mounting). Only the native side is tested: no production screen, no network, no prompt. View libraries (moyasar-apple-pay, nano-icons) are WP2.

### Where it lives

| | |
|---|---|
| `apps/platform/src/lib/native/dev/smoke-checks.ts` | the 36 checks, each requiring its library when it runs; only the smoke app carries it (below) |
| `apps/platform/src/lib/native/dev/smoke.ts` | the runner: `smokeModules` (the ids) and `runSmoke`, which logs one line per module |
| `apps/platform/src/lib/native/dev/smoke-log.ts`, `smoke-bytes.ts` | the line format and the byte helpers (no React Native import, so the harness's tests load them) |
| `apps/platform/src/routes/__dev/smoke/+mobile.svelte` | the lab page `/__dev/smoke` (`?run=1` starts it; not on the lab menu, so the parity references stay) |
| `apps/platform/src/lib/native/dev/Stress.svelte` | the stress scenario `smoke` (`rasheed-svelte:///__dev?run=smoke`; not part of `quick`) |
| `packages/svelte-native/src/native-modules.ts` | `turboModule(name)` and `nativeModule(name)`: how the checks reach a module without its library's JavaScript (the foreign wrappers use the same lookup) |
| `apps/platform/qa/smoke-modules.json`, `qa/smoke-packages.cjs` | the module table and the packages only the smoke app links, read by `app.config.ts` and `react-native.config.js` |
| `apps/platform/qa/commands/smoke.ts`, `ios-smoke.ts`, `qa/parse/smoke.ts`, `qa/lib/smoke.ts`, `qa/lib/variant.ts` | `native-qa smoke` and `build --variant smoke` |
| `apps/platform/qa/gates.json` | `smoke.modules` (the pinned list per platform), the gates `smoke.failed` and `smoke.missing` (their own lane), the run kind `smoke` of each device |

Each line is `[Svelte smoke] <module> ok|fail|skip path=js|direct|js+direct|none ms=<n> <detail>`, then `[Svelte smoke] complete {"os":…,"total":…,"ok":…,"fail":…,"skip":…}`. The path is how the call was made: `js` is the library's own JavaScript API; `direct` is its TurboModule or NativeModule looked up by name, used where the library's JavaScript reaches another module than the one under test or needs React. A check that never settles fails after 10 s. A platform-specific module logs `skip` with the reason on the other platform.

### Three apps: the product, the QA app, the smoke app

The QA app `io.rasheed.svelteqa` is the build every gate measures (boot, frames, parity, accessibility, gestures, crash volume), so it must stay what the product is: it links exactly the native modules of commit `c75c31491`, before the smoke test. The libraries of the smoke test live in a third app, built from the same code with `APP_VARIANT=smoke`:

| | product | QA app | smoke app |
|---|---|---|---|
| `APP_VARIANT` | `production` | none | `smoke` |
| application id (Android and iOS) | `io.rasheed.app` | `io.rasheed.svelteqa` | `io.rasheed.svelteqa.smoke` |
| name, URL scheme | Rasheed, `rasheed` | Rasheed Svelte QA, `rasheed-svelte` | Svelte smoke, `rasheed-svelte-smoke` |
| the 31 smoke-only packages (`smokeOnlyPackages`, `qa/smoke-packages.cjs`) | excluded | excluded | linked |
| smoke config plugins, fake Firebase files, Huawei repository | no | no | yes |
| `libs/samsungpay.jar` needed to build | no | no | yes |
| lab (`/__dev`) | off | on | on |
| smoke test (`/__dev/smoke`, scenario `smoke`) | off | refuses (a note, `info` verdict) | on |

`smokeOnlyPackages` is every package of the table except the six the product already has (secure store, localization, constants, asset, system UI, splash screen) and Statsig, which is JavaScript only. Four things keep them out of the product and the QA app:

- **Expo's autolinker.** `app.config.ts` gives the Svelte native plugin `autolinkingExclude` (one list for the smoke test's libraries and the QA-only modules, see "QA-only modules" below): `smokeOnlyPackages` for every app but the smoke app, with the QA-only modules added for the product, and nothing for the smoke app. The plugin writes the list into `settings.gradle` (`expoAutolinking.exclude`, and `--exclude` on the React Native config command, next to the headless excludes) and into the `Podfile` (`use_expo_modules!(exclude:)` and the same `--exclude` on `expo-modules-autolinking react-native-config`). Expo modules (haptics, location, notifications, …) are only excluded this way.
- **React Native's community autolinker.** `react-native.config.js` (an ES module, like the package) disables the same packages on both platforms unless `APP_VARIANT=smoke`. It covers `EXPO_USE_COMMUNITY_AUTOLINKING=1` and `react-native config`, and alone already empties the React Native list of the 17 non-Expo libraries. The production app does the same for the Huawei library per variant.
- **Config plugins.** `smokePlugins` in `app.config.ts` is empty unless `APP_VARIANT=smoke`; so are `android.googleServicesFile` and `ios.googleServicesFile`.
- **The JavaScript bundle.** The smoke page and runner `require` the libraries statically, so Metro would bundle thirty libraries, the whole `react-native` index their imports reach and Statsig's 87 modules into every app. `metro.config.cjs` gives `withSvelteNative` (`packages/svelte-native/src/metro.ts`) `stubbedPackages` (every non-shared package of the table, `smokeOnlyBundlePackages`: the autolinking list plus Statsig) and `stubbedFiles` (`smoke-checks.ts`) unless `APP_VARIANT=smoke`. Metro resolves each package and every path inside it to a one-line stub (`.svelte-native/stubs/<package>.js`, written when the config loads) that asks a shared core for a Proxy: importing it is harmless (`__esModule` is false), reading anything else, calling or constructing it throws `<package> is not in this app (APP_VARIANT=…)`. A stub only enters a bundle if some code still requires it.
